--- word ---
term: 커피
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: coffee
notes: A loanword from English.
--- word ---
term: 한
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: one
notes: The native Korean number 'one' used before a counter noun.
--- word ---
term: 잔이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 잔 + -이
meaning: cup (subject)
notes: 잔 (jan) is a counter for cups or glasses. -이 (i) is the subject particle, indicating 'a cup' is the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 그렇게
type: adverb
breakdown: 그러하다 + -게
meaning: that much, so
notes: Derived from 그렇다 (geureota, 'to be so'), 그렇게 (geureoke) means 'like that,' 'so much,' or 'to that extent.' It intensifies the following adjective or verb, here 'healing.'
--- word ---
term: 힐링이란
type: noun + particle + ending
breakdown: 힐링 + -이라 + -는
meaning: is a healing (thing)
notes: 힐링 (healing) is a loanword meaning 'healing' or 'relaxation.' -이라 (ira) is the informal form of the copula 이다 (ida, 'to be') attached to a noun. -는 (neun) is a topic particle, often contracted from -이라는 (iraneun), meaning 'that which is called/is a...'. It emphasizes the nature of coffee as 'a healing thing.'
--- word ---
term: 말이지
type: phrase/ending
breakdown: 말이다 + -지
meaning: you know, I mean
notes: A common informal conversational ending. It's a contraction of 말이다 (marida, 'it is to say') + -지 (ji, a confirmation/exclamatory ending). It adds a nuance of 'you know what I mean?' or 'I'm telling you.'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(이)란 말이다
meaning: Used to express 'it is to say that...' or 'it means that...' Often used to explain or emphasize something. The -지 ending adds a conversational, 'you know' feel.
example: 그게 사랑이란 말이지. (That's what love is, you know.)

=== [34:27] 나만의 약속을 했었는데 까먹었다 ===
meaning: I had made a promise to myself, but I forgot.
tone: Informal, reflective, slightly regretful, as Hyejin realizes she broke a personal rule about coffee.
--- word ---
term: 나만의
type: pronoun + particle + particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + -만 (only) + -의 (possessive particle)
meaning: my own / only mine
notes: 나 (na) is the informal first-person pronoun 'I'. -만 (man) is a particle meaning 'only' or 'just'. -의 (ui) is the possessive particle, equivalent to 'of' or 's in English. When combined, 나만의 means 'my own' or 'only mine'. This construction emphasizes exclusivity or individuality. It is informal.
--- word ---
term: 약속을
type: noun + object particle
breakdown: 약속 (promise) + -을 (object particle)
meaning: promise (object)
notes: 약속 (yak-sok) means 'promise' or 'appointment'. -을 (eul) is the object particle, used after nouns ending in a consonant to mark them as the direct object of a verb. This is standard usage.
--- word ---
term: 했었는데
type: verb + past tense + connective ending
breakdown: 하다 (to do) + -았/었- (past tense) + -는데 (connective ending)
meaning: had done, but / had made, but
notes: 하다 (ha-da) means 'to do' or 'to make'. The past tense stem is 했 (haet-). -는데 (neun-de) is a versatile connective ending that provides background information, expresses contrast, or sets the stage for the following clause. Here, it implies 'I had made a promise, but (something happened/I forgot)'. It is an informal ending, suitable for casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 까먹었다
type: verb + past tense + declarative ending
breakdown: 까먹다 (to forget) + -았/었- (past tense) + -다 (declarative ending)
meaning: forgot
notes: 까먹다 (kka-meok-da) means 'to forget' or 'to slip one's mind'. It often implies forgetting something simple or something one should have remembered. The past tense form is 까먹었 (kka-meo-geot-). -다 (da) is an informal declarative ending, used to state a fact. This is a casual way to express forgetting something.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -았/었- + -는데
meaning: This pattern connects two clauses, indicating that the first clause provides background information or a preceding event, often with a subtle nuance of contrast or expectation that is then not met in the second clause. It can be translated as 'did X, but...' or 'was X-ing, and then...'.
example: 밥을 먹었는데 배고파요. (I ate, but I'm hungry.)
level: Intermediate

=== [34:30] 알았어 ===
meaning: Okay / Got it.
tone: Informal, acknowledging, responsive.
--- word ---
term: 알았어
type: verb + past tense + informal ending
breakdown: 알다 (to know/understand) + -았/었- (past tense) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: Okay / Got it
notes: 알다 (al-da) means 'to know' or 'to understand'. 알았어 (a-rat-sseo) is the informal past tense form, commonly used to mean 'I understand', 'Okay', or 'Got it'. It's a very common and versatile expression in casual conversation, used to acknowledge information or agree to something. The formal equivalent would be 알겠습니다 (al-get-sseum-ni-da).

=== [34:39] 위가 안 좋아져? ===
meaning: Does your stomach get bad?
tone: Informal, questioning, concerned, likely in response to a fan's comment about coffee.
--- word ---
term: 위가
type: noun + subject particle
breakdown: 위 (stomach) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: stomach (subject)
notes: 위 (wi) means 'stomach' or 'upper part'. -가 (ga) is the subject particle, used after nouns ending in a vowel to mark them as the subject of the sentence. This is standard usage.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is a short-form negative adverb placed directly before a verb or adjective to negate it. It means 'not'. For example, 안 좋다 (an jo-ta) means 'not good'. The long-form negation uses -지 않다 (ji an-ta).
--- word ---
term: 좋아져?
type: adjective + verb ending + interrogative ending
breakdown: 좋다 (to be good) + -아지다 (to become) + -어? (informal interrogative ending)
meaning: does it become bad? / does it get unwell?
notes: 좋다 (jo-ta) means 'to be good'. The suffix -아지다 (a-ji-da) is attached to adjectives to transform them into verbs meaning 'to become (adjective)'. So, 좋아지다 (jo-a-ji-da) means 'to become good' or 'to get better'. When combined with 안 (an), 안 좋아지다 means 'to become not good' or 'to get worse/unwell'. -어? (eo?) is an informal interrogative ending, used to ask a question in casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective + -아/어지다
meaning: This grammatical pattern is used to express a change of state, meaning 'to become (adjective)' or 'to get (adjective)'. It turns an adjective into a verb.
example: 날씨가 추워졌어요. (The weather became cold.)
level: Beginner

=== [34:49] 만난 거? ===
meaning: Meeting (someone/something)?
tone: Informal, questioning, curious, likely asking for clarification from a fan.
--- word ---
term: 만난
type: verb + adnominal ending
breakdown: 만나다 (to meet) + -ㄴ (adnominal ending)
meaning: met / that met
notes: 만나다 (man-na-da) means 'to meet'. -ㄴ (n) is an adnominal ending attached to verb stems to modify a following noun, indicating a past action. Here, it implies 'the thing/person that was met' or 'having met'. This form is often used to create noun phrases from verbs.
--- word ---
term: 거?
type: noun + interrogative ending (contracted)
breakdown: 것 (thing/fact) + -이 (subject particle, omitted) + -어? (informal interrogative ending, implied)
meaning: the thing? / is it that?
notes: 거 (geo) is a colloquial contraction of 것 (geot), meaning 'thing' or 'fact'. The question mark implies the informal interrogative ending -어? (eo?), making it a question. The full form would be 만난 것이야? (man-nan geo-si-ya?) or 만난 거예요? (man-nan geo-ye-yo?) in slightly more polite informal speech. This is a very casual way to ask 'Is it the thing that was met?' or 'Did you meet (it)?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -ㄴ/은 것
meaning: This pattern nominalizes a verb, turning a verb phrase into a noun phrase. -ㄴ/은 is an adnominal ending for past tense verbs. It means 'the thing that (verb)ed' or 'the fact that (verb)ed'. '것' is often contracted to '거' in casual speech.
example: 어제 본 영화 (the movie I watched yesterday)
level: Beginner

=== [35:03] 사실 어제 친구가 이제 와가지고 ===

type: noun/adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: actually, in fact
notes: 사실 (sasil) is used to introduce a factual statement or to correct a misunderstanding. It can be used as a noun meaning 'fact' or an adverb meaning 'actually'.
--- word ---
term: 어제
type: noun/adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: yesterday
notes: 어제 (eoje) functions as both a noun (yesterday) and an adverb (on yesterday). Similar to 오늘 (oneul), it doesn't require a particle when used as a time adverb.
--- word ---
term: 친구가
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 친구 + -가
meaning: friend (subject)
notes: 친구 (chingu) means 'friend'. -가 (-ga) is the subject particle, used when the noun ends in a vowel. It marks the preceding noun as the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, at this point
notes: 이제 (ije) refers to 'now' in the sense of 'from this point onward' or 'at this stage'. It often implies a change in situation or a transition, unlike 지금 (jigeum) which means 'right now' or 'at this very moment'.
--- word ---
term: 와가지고
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 오다 + -아/어가지고
meaning: came, so/because
notes: This is a colloquial contraction of 와서 (waseo) or 와 가지고 (wa gajigo). The base verb is 오다 (oda) 'to come'. The ending -아/어가지고 (-a/eogajigo) indicates a reason or cause, similar to -아/어서 (-a/eoseo), but often implies a more direct consequence or a chain of events. It's common in informal spoken Korean.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어가지고
meaning: This ending connects two clauses, indicating that the first clause is the reason or cause for the second clause. It's similar to '-아/어서' but often implies a more direct consequence or a sequence of events. It is primarily used in spoken, informal contexts.
example: 배고파가지고 밥 먹었어요. (I was hungry, so I ate.)

=== [35:11] 한방통닭이 너무 먹고 싶은 거야 ===

tone: Informal, expressive, stating a desire.
--- word ---
term: 한방통닭이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 한방통닭 + -이
meaning: hanbang tongdak (subject)
notes: 한방통닭 (hanbang tongdak) is a Korean dish of whole chicken roasted with medicinal herbs. -이 (-i) is the subject particle, used when the noun ends in a consonant. It marks the preceding noun as the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: too much, very
notes: 너무 (neomu) means 'too much' or 'excessively'. In modern colloquial Korean, it is often used simply to mean 'very' or 'really' without a negative connotation, as seen here.
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: verb stem + ending
breakdown: 먹다 + -고
meaning: eat and
notes: 먹다 (meokda) is the verb 'to eat'. -고 (-go) is a connective ending. Here, it's used as part of the '-고 싶다' (go sipda) grammar pattern, which expresses desire.
--- word ---
term: 싶은
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 싶다 + -은
meaning: wanting (to)
notes: 싶다 (sipda) is an auxiliary adjective meaning 'to want to'. When combined with a verb stem + -고, it forms the 'want to do' construction. -은 (-eun) is a descriptive adjective ending used to modify a noun that follows. Here, it modifies the implied '것' (geot) or 'thing'.
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: noun + ending
breakdown: 것 + -이야
meaning: it's that (thing)
notes: This is a colloquial contraction of 것 (geot) 'thing' + 이에요/예요 (ieyo/yeyo) 'is/are'. It's often used to explain a fact, express a realization, or state something casually. It can also be used to express future tense or intention, but here it's explanatory.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고 싶다
meaning: This pattern is attached to a verb stem to express the speaker's desire or wish to perform an action. It means 'to want to do (something)'.
example: 한국에 가고 싶어요. (I want to go to Korea.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는/은/을 거야
meaning: This is a colloquial way to state a fact, explain a situation, or express a future intention. It's a contraction of '-는/은/을 것이다'. The choice of -는, -은, or -을 depends on whether the preceding word is a verb (present/future), adjective (present), or noun (future/explanation). Here, it's used for explanation.
example: 내일 비가 올 거야. (It's going to rain tomorrow.) / 그 사람은 학생인 거야. (It's that he's a student.)
level: Intermediate

=== [35:21] 한방통닭 선물해주면 안 돼? ===
meaning: Can't you give me hanbang tongdak as a present?
tone: Informal, asking for a favor, slightly pleading.
--- word ---
term: 한방통닭
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: hanbang tongdak
notes: Repeated from previous segments, referring to the desired dish.
--- word ---
term: 선물해주면
type: noun + verb + ending
breakdown: 선물 + 하다 + -아/어 주다 + -면
meaning: if you give (as a present)
notes: 선물하다 (seonmulhada) means 'to give a present'. It's formed by 선물 (seonmul) 'present' + 하다 (hada) 'to do'. -아/어 주다 (-a/eo juda) is an auxiliary verb construction meaning 'to do (a favor) for someone'. -면 (-myeon) is a conditional ending meaning 'if' or 'when'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is a short-form negative adverb placed before a verb or adjective to negate it, meaning 'not'.
--- word ---
term: 돼?
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 되다 + -어?
meaning: is it okay?/can it be?
notes: 되다 (doeda) means 'to become, to be possible, to be okay'. -어 (-eo) is the informal interrogative ending. The full, polite form would be 안 돼요? (an dwaeyo?). The combination '-면 안 돼?' (-myeon an dwae?) is a common way to ask 'Is it okay if you don't...?' or 'Can't you...?' implying a request or suggestion.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어 주다
meaning: This auxiliary verb construction is attached to a verb stem to indicate that the action is done as a favor for someone else. It means 'to do (something) for someone'.
example: 문 좀 열어 주세요. (Please open the door for me.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -면 안 되다
meaning: This pattern expresses prohibition or indicates that something is not allowed or not okay. It literally means 'if (you do something), it's not okay'. It can also be used in questions to ask if something is not allowed or if a request cannot be fulfilled.
example: 여기서 담배 피우면 안 돼요. (You shouldn't smoke here.)
level: Beginner

=== [35:23] 그랬어 ===
meaning: I said that.
tone: Informal, concluding a statement, recounting.
--- word ---
term: 그랬어
type: verb
breakdown: 그러다 + -었어
meaning: did that, said that
notes: 그랬어 (geuraesseo) is the informal past tense of 그러다 (geureoda), which means 'to do so', 'to be so', or 'to say so'. In this context, it means 'I said that' or 'I did that (action of asking)'. The speaker is recounting her past action.

=== [35:26] 우리 집 동네에 맛있는 한방통닭 집이 있거든 ===
meaning: Because there's a delicious hanbang tongdak place in my neighborhood.
tone: Informal, explanatory, providing context.
--- word ---
term: 우리
type: pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: our, my
notes: 우리 (uri) means 'we' or 'our'. In Korean culture, it's often used to refer to something personal but shared, like 'my house' (우리 집) or 'my country' (우리나라), implying a sense of community or belonging, even when referring to a singular possession.
--- word ---
term: 집
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: house, home
notes: 집 (jip) means 'house' or 'home'. When combined with 우리 (uri), it means 'my house' or 'our house'.
--- word ---
term: 동네에
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 동네 + -에
meaning: in the neighborhood
notes: 동네 (dongne) means 'neighborhood' or 'town'. -에 (-e) is a location particle indicating 'at', 'in', or 'on' a place.
--- word ---
term: 맛있는
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 맛있다 + -는
meaning: delicious
notes: 맛있다 (masitda) means 'to be delicious'. -는 (-neun) is a present descriptive adjective ending used to modify a noun that follows. For most descriptive verbs (adjectives), -은/는 (-eun/neun) is used, but for 있다/없다 verbs, -는 is used for present tense modification.
--- word ---
term: 한방통닭
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: hanbang tongdak
notes: The specific dish Hyejin wanted.
--- word ---
term: 집이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 집 + -이
meaning: house, place (subject)
notes: 집 (jip) here refers to a 'restaurant' or 'place' that sells 한방통닭 (hanbang tongdak), not necessarily a residential house. -이 (-i) is the subject particle.
--- word ---
term: 있거든
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 있다 + -거든
meaning: there is, because there is
notes: 있다 (itda) means 'to exist' or 'there is'. -거든 (-geodeun) is a sentence-ending particle used to provide background information or a reason/justification for a previous statement or question. It implies 'because (you asked/I said that), (the reason is)...'. It's commonly used in informal conversation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는 (present descriptive modifier)
meaning: This ending is attached to verb stems (including 있다/없다) to modify a following noun, indicating that the noun is currently performing the action or is in the described state. For descriptive verbs (adjectives), -은/는 is used, but for action verbs and 있다/없다, -는 is used for the present tense.
example: 책을 읽는 사람 (the person reading a book) / 재미있는 영화 (an interesting movie)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -거든
meaning: This sentence-ending particle is used to provide a reason, background information, or justification for a previous statement or question. It implies 'because' or 'since' in a conversational context, often when the speaker assumes the listener is curious or needs more context.
example: 지금 가야 해. 바쁘거든. (I have to go now. Because I'm busy.)
level: Intermediate

=== [35:29] 그니까 오는 길에 좀 사와줄 수 없는 집 ===
meaning: So, [it was a place/situation where] they couldn't buy and bring it on the way?
tone: Conversational, recounting a past difficulty, slightly rhetorical.
--- word ---
term: 그니까
type: conjunction
breakdown: 그러니까
meaning: So, I mean
notes: This is a colloquial contraction of 그러니까 (geureonikka). It's used to connect ideas, explain, or elaborate on a previous statement. It sets a conversational and informal tone.
--- word ---
term: 오는
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 오다 + -는
meaning: coming, on the way
notes: 오다 (oda) means 'to come'. The adnominal ending -는 (-neun) attaches to a verb stem to describe a noun that is currently performing the action. Here, it modifies 길 (gil, way/road), meaning 'the way that is coming' or 'on the way here'. This is informal speech.
--- word ---
term: 길에
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 길 + -에
meaning: on the way
notes: 길 (gil) means 'way' or 'road'. -에 (-e) is a locative particle indicating 'at' or 'on'. The phrase 오는 길에 (oneun gire) is a common expression meaning 'on the way here' or 'while coming'.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a bit, please
notes: 좀 (jom) is an adverb that softens a request or statement, or adds a slight emphasis. It can mean 'a little', 'a bit', or 'please'. It's commonly used in informal conversation.
--- word ---
term: 사와줄
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 사다 + 오다 + 주다 + -ㄹ
meaning: buy and bring for me
notes: This is a complex verb construction: 사다 (sada, to buy) + 오다 (oda, to come/bring) + 주다 (juda, to give/do for someone) + -ㄹ (-l, future/potential adnominal ending). It expresses the action of buying something and bringing it for the benefit of the speaker. This is informal speech.
--- word ---
term: 수
type: dependent noun
breakdown: 
meaning: ability, possibility
notes: 수 (su) is a dependent noun used with the -ㄹ/을 수 있다/없다 (-l/eul su itda/eopda) grammar pattern to express possibility or ability ('can' / 'cannot').
--- word ---
term: 없는
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 없다 + -는
meaning: not existing, cannot
notes: 없다 (eopda) means 'to not exist' or 'to not have'. The adnominal ending -는 (-neun) modifies the following noun, indicating that the noun 'does not exist' or 'is not possible' in this context. This is informal speech.
--- word ---
term: 집
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: place, store
notes: While 집 (jip) typically means 'house' or 'home', in contexts involving buying food or services, it can colloquially refer to a 'store', 'shop', or 'establishment'. Here, it likely refers to a restaurant or shop that sells the hanbang tongdak (roasted chicken) Hyejin is talking about, implying a difficulty in getting it.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아/어 주다
meaning: To do something for someone's benefit. It shows a favor or service.
example: 도와주세요. (Please help me.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -ㄹ/을 수 있다/없다
meaning: To be able to / unable to do something; to be possible / impossible.
example: 한국어를 할 수 있어요. (I can speak Korean.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -는/ㄴ/은/ㄹ/을 Noun (Adnominal Endings)
meaning: A verb is conjugated with an adnominal ending to modify a noun, functioning like an adjective clause. -는 is for present tense verbs.
example: 먹는 사람 (the person who eats)
level: Beginner/Intermediate

=== [35:33] 그래서 진짜? 알겠어 ===

tone: Conversational, informal, accepting.
--- word ---
term: 그래서

breakdown: 
meaning: So, therefore
notes: 그래서 (geuraeseo) connects sentences, indicating a result or continuation from the previous statement. It's a common conversational connector.
--- word ---
term: 진짜?
type: adverb/interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: really?, for real?
notes: 진짜 (jinjja) can function as an adverb meaning 'really' or 'truly', or as an interjection expressing surprise, disbelief, or seeking confirmation. The rising intonation makes it a question. It's very common in informal speech.
--- word ---
term: 알겠어
type: verb ending
breakdown: 알다 + -겠어
meaning: Okay, I got it.
notes: 알다 (alda, to know) + -겠어 (-gesseo), an informal ending. While -겠- often indicates future tense or speaker's intention, in this context, it expresses understanding or acceptance, similar to 'I understand' or 'Okay'. This is informal speech.

meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention, will, or assumption. In informal speech (-겠어), it can also convey understanding or acceptance.
example: 제가 하겠습니다. (I will do it.) 알겠습니다. (I understand/Okay.)
level: Beginner

=== [35:35] 그래서 친구가 이제 한 마리를 사왔어 ===
meaning: So, my friend bought one now.
tone: Conversational, narrative, informal.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: So, therefore
notes: 그래서 (geuraeseo) connects sentences, indicating a result or continuation from the previous statement. It's a common conversational connector.
--- word ---
term: 친구가
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 친구 + -가
meaning: friend (subject)
notes: 친구 (chingu) means 'friend'. -가 (-ga) is the subject particle, indicating that 'friend' is the subject of the sentence. This is informal speech.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, from now on
notes: 이제 (ije) refers to a point in time, often indicating a change or the start of a new phase. It can mean 'now' or 'from now on'. It differs from 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now, at this very moment'. This is informal speech.
--- word ---
term: 한
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: one
notes: 한 (han) is the pure Korean numeral for 'one', used before a counter. It's part of the counting system for specific items.
--- word ---
term: 마리를
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 마리 + -를
meaning: (one) animal (object)
notes: 마리 (mari) is a counter for animals. -를 (-reul) is the object particle. In this context, given Hyejin's discussion about 'hanbang tongdak' (roasted chicken) from the Story Bible, 'one' refers to one whole chicken, which is counted using 마리. This is informal speech.
--- word ---
term: 사왔어
type: verb
breakdown: 사다 + 오다 + -았어
meaning: bought and brought
notes: This is a contraction of 사 가지고 왔어 (sa gajigo wasseo). 사다 (sada, to buy) + 오다 (oda, to come/bring) + -았어 (-asseo, past tense informal ending). It means 'bought and came (with it)', or simply 'brought after buying'. This is informal speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아/어 오다
meaning: To do something and come, often implying bringing something or doing something and returning. It indicates an action performed and completed, with the result brought back.
example: 책을 사 왔어요. (I bought a book and brought it.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: Pure Korean Numerals + Counter
meaning: Korean uses specific counters for different types of nouns (e.g., people, animals, objects). Pure Korean numerals (하나, 둘, 셋...) change form when used with counters (한, 두, 세...).
example: 한 개 (one item), 두 명 (two people), 세 마리 (three animals).
level: Beginner

meaning: but, however, by the way

term: 이제

meaning: now, from now on

notes: 일어나다 (ireonada) means 'to wake up' or 'to get up'. -았/었- (at/eot) is the past tense marker. -는데도 (neundedo) is a connective ending meaning 'even though' or 'despite'. It expresses a contrast or unexpected result. This is an intermediate grammar pattern.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a little, a bit
notes: A colloquial contraction of 조금 (jogeum). It's commonly used in informal speech to soften a statement or indicate a small degree. It can also mean 'please' when used with imperatives (e.g., 좀 주세요 - Please give me some).

breakdown: 느낌 + ?
meaning: feeling?

--- grammar ---

meaning: This ending means 'even though' or 'despite'. It connects two clauses where the second clause expresses an unexpected result or contrast to the first.

=== [35:57] 아니 뭐라 그래야 되지? ===
meaning: No, what should I say?
tone: Informal, rhetorical, searching for the right words.
--- word ---
term: 아니
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: no, well
notes: 아니 (ani) is an interjection meaning 'no'. It can also be used to express surprise, disagreement, or to simply start a sentence, similar to 'well' or 'no, wait' in English. Here, Hyejin uses it to pause and rephrase.
--- word ---
term: 뭐라
type: adverbial contraction
breakdown: 무어라고
meaning: what (should I say)
notes: A common colloquial contraction of 무어라고 (mueorago), which means 'what (to say/do)'. It's often used with verbs like 하다 (hada - to do/say) or 부르다 (bureuda - to call) to ask 'what should I say/call it?'.
--- word ---
term: 그래야
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 그렇다 + -아야
meaning: should say/do (like that)
notes: 그렇다 (geureota) means 'to be so, to be like that'. The -아야/어야 (aya/eoya) ending expresses necessity or obligation, meaning 'must/should do'. So, 그래야 (geuraeya) means 'must be like that' or 'must say so'. In this context, it's part of the rhetorical question 'what should I say?'.
--- word ---
term: 되지?
type: verb + ending + question mark
breakdown: 되다 + -지?
meaning: should I do/say? (rhetorical)
notes: 되다 (doeda) means 'to become, to be possible, to be done'. The -지? (ji?) ending is used to confirm something or to ask a rhetorical question, often implying 'right?' or 'isn't it?'. Here, it combines with 그래야 to form a rhetorical question about what the appropriate expression would be.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어야 되다/하다
meaning: This pattern expresses necessity or obligation, meaning 'must do' or 'should do'. It indicates that an action is required or advisable.
example: 공부해야 돼요. (I have to study.)
level: Beginner

term: 아직도
type: adverb + particle
breakdown: 아직 + 도
meaning: still, yet
notes: 아직 (ajik) means 'still' or 'yet'. 도 (do) is an additive particle meaning 'also' or 'even'. When combined, 아직도 (ajikdo) emphasizes that something is 'still' happening or 'still' in a certain state, often implying surprise or a longer duration than expected.

=== [36:04] 오 맞아 있어 그치? ===
meaning: Oh, that's right, it's there, right?
tone: Informal, confirming, seeking agreement from the audience.
--- word ---
term: 오
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: oh
notes: 오 (o) is an interjection similar to 'oh' in English, expressing realization, surprise, or agreement. It's a very common and informal expression.
--- word ---
term: 맞아
type: descriptive verb (adjective)
breakdown: 맞다
meaning: that's right, correct
notes: 맞다 (matda) means 'to be correct' or 'to be right'. 맞아 (maja) is the informal, plain form, used to express agreement or confirmation. It's a very common conversational filler.
--- word ---
term: 있어
type: verb
breakdown: 있다
meaning: it is (there), exists

--- word ---
term: 그치?
type: ending + question mark
breakdown: 그렇다 + -지?
meaning: right? isn't it?
notes: A colloquial contraction of 그렇지? (geureoji?), which comes from 그렇다 (geureota - to be so) + -지? (ji?). The -지? ending is used to seek agreement or confirmation from the listener, similar to 'right?' or 'isn't it?' in English. It's very common in informal speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -지?
meaning: This ending is used to seek agreement or confirmation from the listener. It can also be used rhetorically or to express a mild suggestion.
example: 이거 맛있지? (This is delicious, right?)
level: Beginner

=== [36:14] 너무 맛있게 먹고 싶지? ===
meaning: You want to eat something really delicious, right?
tone: Informal, engaging, suggestive, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, too much
notes: 너무 (neomu) means 'very' or 'too much'. While traditionally meaning 'too much' (often with a negative connotation), in modern colloquial Korean, it's frequently used simply as an intensifier for 'very' or 'really', even with positive adjectives, as seen here.
--- word ---
term: 맛있게
type: descriptive verb + ending
breakdown: 맛있다 + -게
meaning: deliciously
notes: 맛있다 (masitda) means 'to be delicious'. The -게 (ge) ending transforms a descriptive verb into an adverb, meaning 'in a ... way'. So, 맛있게 (masitge) means 'deliciously'. It modifies the verb 먹고 싶다 (meokgo sipda - want to eat).
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: verb + conjunction
breakdown: 먹다 + -고
meaning: eat and (then)
notes: 먹다 (meokda) means 'to eat'. The -고 (go) ending connects verbs or adjectives, often indicating a sequence of actions ('and then') or simply listing items. Here, it connects 'eat' to 'want'.
--- word ---
term: 싶지?
type: verb + ending + question mark
breakdown: 싶다 + -지?
meaning: want to, right?
notes: 싶다 (sipda) is a verb meaning 'to want to' when attached to another verb stem (e.g., 먹고 싶다 - want to eat). The -지? (ji?) ending, as discussed, seeks agreement. So, 싶지? (sipji?) means 'you want to, right?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고 싶다
meaning: This pattern expresses a desire or wish to do something, meaning 'to want to do'. It is attached to the stem of a verb.
example: 한국에 가고 싶어요. (I want to go to Korea.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -게 (adverbial ending)
meaning: This ending transforms a descriptive verb (adjective) into an adverb, indicating 'in a ... way' or 'so that ...'. It modifies a following verb.
example: 예쁘게 입다 (to dress beautifully)
level: Beginner

=== [36:21] 한방통닭 진짜 맛있는데 ===

type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, truly

--- grammar ---
pattern: -는데 / -(으)ㄴ데 / -인데
meaning: This versatile connective ending provides background information, context, or a mild contrast for the following clause. It can be translated as 'but', 'and', 'so', or 'while'.

=== [36:30] 그래 사줘야 된다 ===

breakdown: 사주다 + -어야

The '-어야' ending expresses obligation or necessity, meaning 'must' or 'should'. This is an informal, conversational form.

meaning: it becomes, it is done, it should be

The '-ㄴ다' (nda) ending is an informal, plain form declarative ending used for verbs, common in casual speech and writing.
--- grammar ---

meaning: This pattern expresses obligation or necessity, meaning 'must' or 'should'. It indicates that an action is required or necessary.
example: 공부해야 돼요. (Gongbuhaeya dwaeyo.) = I have to study.

The '-잖아' ending is an informal, conversational ending used to state something the speaker assumes the listener already knows or agrees with, often translated as 'you know, right?' or 'isn't it?'
--- grammar ---

breakdown:

meaning: though I want to, indeed I want to

example: 한국에 가고 싶어요. (Hanguge gago sipeoyo.) = I want to go to Korea.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -긴 하다
meaning: This pattern emphasizes the truth of a statement while often implying a contrast or concession. It can be translated as 'indeed... but' or 'it is true that... but'.
example: 재미있긴 한데 좀 어려워요. (Jaemiitgin hande jom eoryeowoyo.) = It is fun, but it's a bit difficult.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---

meaning: This versatile connective ending provides background information, introduces a contrast, or sets the stage for the following clause. It can mean 'but', 'however', 'and', or 'so'.
example: 비가 오는데 우산이 없어요. (Biga oneunde usani eopseoyo.) = It's raining, but I don't have an umbrella.

term: 한번
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: once, one time, just once

meaning: even if I stop by, if I stop by

term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: too, very, really

breakdown: 좋다 + -(으)ㄹ

notes: The future/presumptive adjectival form of '좋다' (jota, to be good). The '-(으)ㄹ' (eul) ending is attached to adjective stems to modify a following noun, indicating a future state, possibility, or conjecture. Here, it modifies '거' (geo, thing).
--- word ---
term: 거
type: noun
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing
notes: A colloquial contraction of '것' (geot), a dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'fact'. It's often used in conjunction with adjectival verb forms to create noun phrases, as in '-(으)ㄹ 것 같다' (eul geot gatda, it seems like it will be).
--- word ---
term: 같은데
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 같다 + -ㄴ데
meaning: it seems like, but
notes: Combines '같다' (gatda, to be like/the same) with the connective ending '-ㄴ데' (nde). The full expression '-(으)ㄹ 것 같다' means 'it seems like it will be'. Adding '-ㄴ데' here implies a trailing thought, a wish that might be difficult to fulfill, or setting up a contrast, similar to 'it would be good, but...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는 길에
meaning: This pattern means 'on the way to (doing something)' or 'while going to (a place)'. It indicates an action performed during a journey.
example: 집에 가는 길에 빵을 샀어요. (Jibe ganeun gire ppangeul sasseoyo.) = I bought bread on the way home.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어도
meaning: This pattern expresses concession, meaning 'even if', 'even though', or 'regardless of'. It can also imply possibility or permission in certain contexts.
example: 바빠도 밥은 먹어야 해요. (Bappado babeun meogeoya haeyo.) = Even if you're busy, you should eat.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ 것 같다
meaning: This pattern expresses conjecture or probability, meaning 'it seems like it will be', 'I think it will be', or 'it looks like'.
example: 비가 올 것 같아요. (Biga ol geot gatayo.) = It looks like it will rain.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는데/-은데/-ㄴ데
meaning: This versatile connective ending provides background information, introduces a contrast, or sets the stage for the following clause. It can mean 'but', 'however', 'and', or 'so'.
example: 비가 오는데 우산이 없어요. (Biga oneunde usani eopseoyo.) = It's raining, but I don't have an umbrella.

breakdown: 이후 + -에
meaning: after
notes: '이후' (ihu) means 'after' or 'subsequent period'. '-에' (e) is a time particle meaning 'at' or 'in'. So, '이후에' means 'after (a certain time)'.
--- word ---
term: 가면
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 가다 + -(으)면
meaning: if I go, when I go
notes: Combines '가다' (gada, to go) with the conditional ending '-(으)면' (eumyeon/myeon), which means 'if' or 'when'. It introduces a condition for the following clause.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: A short-form negative adverb placed directly before a verb or adjective to negate it. It means 'not' or 'do not'.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: verb
breakdown: 되다
meaning: is it allowed?, is it possible?
notes: The informal, casual form of '되다' (doeda). In the context of '-(으)면 안 되다', it means 'is not allowed' or 'should not'. Here, as a question, '안 돼?' means 'is it not allowed?' or 'can't I?'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)면 안 되다
meaning: This pattern expresses prohibition or that something is not allowed/should not be done. It means 'cannot (do something)' or 'should not (do something)'.
example: 여기서 담배 피우면 안 돼요. (Yeogiseo dambae pi-umyeon an dwaeyo.) = You can't smoke here.
level: Beginner

=== [37:28] 근데 어차피 오늘은 좀 늦게 끝나서 ===
meaning: But anyway, since today ends a bit late...
tone: Informal, conversational, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction/Interjection
breakdown: 근데
meaning: But, by the way
notes: This is a common contraction of 그런데 (geureonde). It's used to introduce a new topic, a contrasting idea, or to continue a thought. It's very common in informal spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 어차피
type: Adverb
breakdown: 어차피
meaning: Anyway, in any case, after all
notes: Used to express that something will happen regardless of other factors or efforts. It can convey a sense of resignation or inevitability. It's suitable for informal to standard contexts.
--- word ---
term: 오늘은
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 오늘 (today) + -은 (topic particle)
meaning: Today (as for today)
notes: 오늘 is a noun meaning 'today'. -은/는 is a topic particle, indicating that 'today' is the topic of the sentence. It often highlights or contrasts the topic with other elements.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: Adverb
breakdown: 좀
meaning: A little, a bit
notes: This is a common contraction of 조금 (jogeum). It's frequently used in spoken Korean to soften requests, express a small degree, or make a statement less direct. It's informal.
--- word ---
term: 늦게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 늦다 (to be late) + -게 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: Late
notes: -게 is an adverbial suffix attached to adjectives or verbs to transform them into adverbs, meaning 'in a ... manner' or 'to ... extent'. Here, the adjective 늦다 (to be late) becomes the adverb 늦게 (late).
--- word ---
term: 끝나서
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 끝나다 (to end, to be over) + -아서/어서 (reason/sequence ending)
meaning: Because it ends, since it ends
notes: -아서/어서 is a connective ending used to indicate cause or reason ('because/since') or a sequence of events ('and then'). The sentence trails off, implying a consequence that will follow from the late ending time. This form is informal.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -아서/어서
meaning: This ending connects two clauses, indicating that the first clause is the reason or cause for the second clause ('because/since'), or that the first action/state leads directly to the second action/state ('and then').
example: 비가 와서 집에 갔어요. (Because it rained, I went home.)
level: Beginner/Intermediate

=== [37:35] 가는 길에 그럼 한번 들릴까? ===

meaning: Once, one time

--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -ㄹ까/-을까?
meaning: This ending is used to ask for the listener's opinion, make a suggestion, or express a self-question. It translates to 'Shall I...?', 'Should I...?', or 'Do you think I should...?'.
example: 우리 영화 볼까? (Shall we watch a movie?)

=== [38:24] 배 소리가 들렸단 말이야? ===

term: 배
type: Noun
breakdown: 배
meaning: Stomach

--- word ---
term: 소리가
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 소리 (sound) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: The sound (as the subject)
notes: -가 is a subject particle, indicating that 'the sound' is the subject of the verb '들리다' (to be heard).
--- word ---
term: 들렸단
type: Verb (past tense, contracted adnominal)
breakdown: 들리다 (to be heard) + -었- (past tense) + -다 (declarative) + -는 (adnominal) -> 들렸다는 (full form) -> 들렸단 (contracted)
meaning: That it was heard, that it rumbled
notes: This is a contraction of 들렸다는. The full form would be 들렸다는 말이야? (You're saying that it was heard?). The pattern -다는 is a common way to quote or report something, often used with 말이야 to mean 'You're saying...?' or 'Is it true that...?'. It's informal.

--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -다는 말이야?
meaning: This pattern is used to confirm something that was just said, often with a nuance of surprise, disbelief, or seeking clarification. It translates to 'Are you saying that...?' or 'Do you mean that...?'. It's a common informal expression.
example: 네가 그걸 다 먹었단 말이야? (You're saying you ate all of that?)

=== [38:44] 이제 슬슬 나는 가볼게 ===
meaning: Now, I'll slowly get going.
tone: Informal, polite departure.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: adverb
breakdown: 이제
meaning: Now / From now on
notes: 이제 (ije) refers to 'now' in the sense of 'from this point forward' or 'at this stage.' It's often contrasted with 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now' or 'at this very moment.' Here, it implies the start of the departure process.
--- word ---
term: 슬슬
type: adverb
breakdown: 슬슬
meaning: Slowly / Gradually / Gently
notes: 슬슬 (seulseul) is an adverb meaning 'slowly,' 'gradually,' 'gently,' or 'stealthily.' It often implies a relaxed or unhurried pace, or a subtle beginning of an action. Here, it softens the departure, making it sound less abrupt.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: pronoun + topic particle
breakdown: 나 + -는
meaning: I (as for me)
notes: 나 (na) is the informal first-person pronoun 'I.' -는 (-neun) is the topic particle, highlighting 'I' as the subject of the upcoming action, often implying a contrast or emphasis (e.g., 'as for me, I'll go').
--- word ---
term: 가볼게
type: verb + auxiliary verb + future/volitional ending
breakdown: 가다 + -아/어 보다 + -ㄹ게
meaning: I'll try to go / I'll get going
notes: 가다 (gada) means 'to go.' -아/어 보다 (-a/eo boda) is an auxiliary verb pattern meaning 'to try doing something' or 'to do something and see.' In the context of going, it often softens the statement, making it 'I'll go and see' or simply 'I'll get going.' -ㄹ게 (-lge) is a volitional ending used when the speaker expresses their intention or promise to do something for the listener's benefit or with the listener's awareness. It's informal. Common learner mistake: Confusing -ㄹ게 with -ㄹ래요. -ㄹ게 is used when the speaker is making a decision or promise to the listener, while -ㄹ래요 is used to ask about the listener's preference or to state the speaker's preference.

pattern: -아서/어서

--- grammar ---
pattern: -은데/는데
meaning: This connective ending provides background information for the following clause, expresses a mild contrast, or sets up a question or suggestion. It can be translated as 'but', 'and', 'so', or 'however'.

type: adjective (truncated)
breakdown: 미안하다
meaning: Sorry...
notes: A truncated, informal form of 미안하다 (to be sorry). This is very common in casual spoken Korean, often used when hesitating or quickly expressing an emotion.
--- word ---
term: 미안하다
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 미안하다 + -다
meaning: I'm sorry.
notes: 미안하다 (to be sorry) is an adjective. The -다 ending is the dictionary form, but it can be used in informal speech for a direct, declarative statement, sometimes with a sense of finality or emphasis. It's less formal than 미안합니다/미안해요.

=== [39:02] 근데 이거는 ===
meaning: But this is...
tone: Informal, transitional, conversational, introducing a new point.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: conjunction (contraction)
breakdown: 그런데
meaning: But, however, by the way
notes: A common contraction of 그런데. It functions as a conjunction meaning 'but', 'however', or 'by the way', used to introduce a new topic, a contrasting idea, or to continue a thought. It's highly colloquial.
--- word ---
term: 이거는
type: pronoun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 이것 + -은
meaning: this (topic)
notes: 이것 (this thing) is a demonstrative pronoun, often contracted to 이거 in casual speech. -은/는 is the topic particle, marking 'this' as the topic of the upcoming statement.

=== [39:03] 인스타 오류라는 얘기가 있으니까 ===
meaning: Since there's talk that it's an Instagram error...
tone: Informal, explanatory, slightly defensive, providing context.
--- word ---
term: 인스타
type: noun (contraction)
breakdown: 인스타그램
meaning: Instagram
notes: A colloquial contraction of 인스타그램 (Instagram). This informal shortening is very common in everyday Korean speech.
--- word ---
term: 오류라는
type: noun + particle + verb ending
breakdown: 오류 + -이라고 하다 + -는
meaning: that it's an error / called an error
notes: This combines 오류 (error) with the quotative particle -이라고 하다 (to say that it is / to be called) and the adnominal ending -는. It means 'the talk/story that it is an error' or 'what is called an error'. The full form would be '오류라고 하는'.
--- word ---
term: 얘기가
type: noun + particle (colloquial)
breakdown: 이야기 + 가
meaning: talk/story (subject)
notes: 얘기 is a colloquial contraction of 이야기 (talk, story, conversation). 가 is the subject particle, indicating 'talk/story' as the subject of the clause.
--- word ---
term: 있으니까
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 있다 + -으니까
meaning: since there is / because there is
notes: 있다 (to exist, to have) is a verb. -으니까/니까 is a connective ending indicating cause or reason, meaning 'since' or 'because'. It explains why she mentioned the previous point (her apology).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(이)라고 하다
meaning: This pattern is used to quote someone's words or to state what something is called or defined as. When used with a noun, it means 'to be called (noun)' or 'to say that it is (noun)'.
example: 이것은 사과라고 해요. (This is called an apple.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -으니까/니까
meaning: This connective ending expresses cause or reason, similar to -아서/어서, but it can be used with imperative (commands) and propositive (suggestions) sentences. It means 'since' or 'because'.
example: 바쁘니까 다음에 만나요. (Since I'm busy, let's meet next time.)
level: Beginner

=== [39:17] 좋은 하루 보내고 ===
meaning: Have a good day (and)...
tone: Informal, friendly, wishing, conversational, implying continuation.
--- word ---
term: 좋은
type: adjective + adnominal ending
breakdown: 좋다 + -은
meaning: good
notes: 좋다 (to be good) is an adjective. -은 is the adnominal ending for adjectives ending in a consonant (or for past tense verbs), modifying the following noun. Here, it means 'good day'.
--- word ---
term: 하루
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: day
notes: A noun meaning 'day'. It is often used in expressions like '좋은 하루 보내세요' (Have a good day).
--- word ---
term: 보내고
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 보내다 + -고
meaning: spend/send (and)
notes: 보내다 (to send, to spend time) is a verb. -고 is a connective ending that links two or more clauses, indicating sequential actions ('and then') or simply listing actions/states ('and'). Here, it's a casual way to wish someone well, implying 'have a good day, and I will too' or 'have a good day, and I'll see you later'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고
meaning: This connective ending links two or more clauses, indicating that the actions or states occur in sequence, simultaneously, or are simply listed. It can be translated as 'and' or 'and then'.
example: 밥을 먹고 영화를 봤어요. (I ate and then watched a movie.)
level: Beginner

type: adjective + adnominal ending
breakdown: 좋다 + -은
meaning: good
notes: 좋다 (to be good) is an adjective. -은 is the adnominal ending, modifying '하루' (day).

meaning: day

meaning: I will spend/have
notes: 보내다 (to send, to spend time) is a verb. -을게/ㄹ게 is a volitional/promissory ending used when the speaker expresses their intention or promise to do something, often for the listener's benefit or in response to the listener's statement/request. Here, Hyejin is responding to her own wish for the fans, saying she will also have a good day.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -을게/ㄹ게
meaning: This ending expresses the speaker's intention or promise to do something, often with the nuance that the action is for the listener's benefit or in response to the listener. It is used only with verbs and in informal speech.
example: 제가 도와줄게요. (I will help you.)
level: Beginner

=== [39:35] 다들 건강 조심하고 ===

breakdown: 
meaning: health

meaning: take care and

--- grammar ---
pattern: -고 (-go)
meaning: Connects two or more clauses or actions in sequence or as a list. It can mean 'and' or 'and then'.
example: 밥 먹고 영화 봤어요. (I ate and then watched a movie.)
level: Beginner

=== [39:38] 알았어? ===
meaning: Got it?
tone: Informal, direct question, checking understanding.
--- word ---
term: 알았어?
type: verb + past tense + interrogative ending
breakdown: 알다 + -았- + -어?
meaning: Got it? / Understood?
notes: This is the informal past tense interrogative form of 알다 (alda), 'to know' or 'to understand'. -았/었- is the past tense marker, and -어? is the informal interrogative ending. It's a common way to ask 'Do you understand?' or 'Did you get that?'. The standard polite form would be 알았어요? (arasseoyo?).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -았/었어? (-at/eosseo?)
meaning: Used to form an informal past tense question. It asks if an action was completed or if something was understood in the past.
example: 밥 먹었어? (Did you eat?)
level: Beginner

=== [39:48] 고마워 ===
meaning: Thank you.
tone: Informal, appreciative, warm.
--- word ---
term: 고마워
type: adjective + informal polite ending
breakdown: 고맙다 + -어
meaning: Thank you
notes: This is the informal polite form of the adjective 고맙다 (gomapda), 'to be thankful'. meaning: A common informal polite ending for verbs and adjectives. It's used in casual conversations with people you are close to or who are younger than you. It's also the base for the standard polite -아요/어요 form.
example: 가다 -> 가. (Go.) / 예쁘다 -> 예뻐. (It's pretty.)

=== [39:55] 안녕 ===

type: greeting/farewell
breakdown: 
meaning: Bye / Hello
notes: 안녕 (annyeong) is a versatile, informal greeting that can mean both 'hello' and 'goodbye'. It is typically used with people of similar age, younger individuals, or those with whom one has a close relationship. The polite form is 안녕하세요 (annyeonghaseyo).
